News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Democrats Keep Looking For the “Next Obama.” They Should Do This Instead.
2026-09-09

Democrats Keep Looking For the “Next Obama.” They Should Do This Instead.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The first time I remember hearing Barack Obama’s name was in late 2006 or early 2007. I was sitting in seventh grade social studies, where we were encouraged to discuss current events. “I think he’s going to run for president,” our teacher confided in a knowing yet slightly hushed tone, almost like she still couldn’t quite believe it. Back then, much of the excitement was bound up in the prospect of America having its first Black president, but Obama ultimately proved himself to be worthy of it. Two decades on, the energy he created has morphed into something decidedly more desperate. Obama is the only Democratic president this century to win reelection and remains one of the country’s most popular political figures. Which might explain why Democrats, looking ahead to congressional elections this fall and a 2028 presidential race whose stakes feel existential, keep looking for the former president’s spiritual successor: someone who can inspire, speak, and above all win, just like the real thing. All summer, voters, consultants, and pundits have repeatedly attached Obama’s name like a Homeric epithet to a bumper crop of young, talented, and mostly male candidates running across the country. It’s happened to Abdul El-Sayed, the party’s nominee for U.S. Senate in Michigan, whose self-confident swagger and immigrant-family story have invited the comparison. “It reminded me a lot of the Barack Obama bio,” Jon Favreau, Obama’s former speechwriter, recalled after seeing El-Sayed at a fundraiser. Then there’s James Talarico, the Texas state representative running for U.S. Senate whose winning primary campaign former New York Times columnist Charles Blow likened to Obama’s 2008 faceoff against Hillary Clinton. After Talarico’s campaign posted footage of the former president stumping with him at a taco shop this spring, the equivalencies flowed in. “I think you have what it takes to be the next Obama and Lord knows we need it!” one Facebook commenter wrote. The Obama parallel has similarly been lavished on two Democratic governors who look set to cruise to reelection this fall: Josh Shapiro of Pennsylvania, and Wes Moore of Maryland, who started fielding Obama analogies even before he became the state’s first Black governor. Pete Buttigieg, the former Biden transportation secretary who isn’t officially running for anything right now, has been getting the comparison for years. Even Graham Platner, the scruffy Maine oyster farmer, was winning Obama-esque praise before his Senate candidacy imploded. Jim Messina, Obama’s 2012 campaign manager, dubbed Platner’s victory speech on primary night a “masterclass” that recalled the 44th president. (A few weeks later, after an ex-girlfriend accused Platner of rape, Messina suggested Platner should “go jump in a Maine lake.”) Further south, Sen. Jon Ossoff, a Georgia Democrat who is up for reelection this fall, has been likened to the former president for viral speeches that are already earning him national attention and fueling 2028 chatter. The search for an Obama inheritor isn’t totally new. Ahead of the 2020 election, Joe Biden, Kamala Harris, and Cory Booker tussled over the former president’s legacy in what the Los Angeles Times perceptively dubbed “the Obama primary.” The search has taken on new proportions and greater urgency as Democrats once again seek to claw their way back to power after yet another Trump presidency. The question is: Even if they find their guy, is an Obama 2.0 what the party needs, now, or in 2028? To give Democrats their due, trying to re-create a popular two-term commander in chief in the aggregate doesn’t seem like the worst strategy in the world. After the Biden interregnum and Donald Trump’s return, nostalgia for better political times appears broadly felt: A January poll of a hypothetical 2028 third-term matchup against Trump found Obama ahead by double digits. (Neither man can legally be elected again.) Historical patterns are worth taking seriously, too. Every couple of decades, it seems, Democrats turn to a sunny, novel-seeming, relatively young man to resuscitate their party’s political fortunes. It happened with John F. Kennedy in 1960, Jimmy Carter in 1976, and Bill Clinton in 1992. And as Obama proved, lesser-known lawmakers with funny names can sometimes go on to make history. But there are a few reasons to wonder whether Democrats’ search for “the next Obama” has gotten a little out of hand—and might inadvertently lead the party even deeper into the political wilderness. For one thing, the comparison has become so common that it’s started to lose meaning, and power. The last few years of American politics are littered with Democrats hailed as the next Obama who ended up falling short. The late PBS anchor Gwen Ifill canonized Kamala Harris as “the female Barack Obama” back in 2009, but Harris’ two presidential campaigns both failed. A 2018 New Yorker article about the presidential prospects of Deval Patrick, the former Massachusetts governor, pronounced him “a kind of political heir to Barack Obama” and quoted former Obama advisers favorably comparing the two men. When Patrick did finally launch a 2020 campaign, it lasted just three months. Obama, of course, isn’t a ghost, and he has been talking up some of the same candidates others are likening to him. Obama called up El-Sayed last month and has publicly praised Talarico, New York Mayor Zohran Mamdani, and other promising young Democrats. Still, not even Obama’s Obamadar is perfect. In 2018, as Beto O’Rourke was weighing a national campaign, the former president was the one drawing comparisons between them. “The reason I was able to make a connection with a sizable portion of the country was because people had a sense that I said what I meant,” Obama said then, making clear that he felt O’Rourke shared that special touch. Former Obama aides reportedly called O’Rourke’s people to offer their help, remarking that the lanky Texan reminded them of their former boss. “He’s Barack Obama, but white,” one Democratic bundler told Politico. Judging by polling and fundraising, though, the rest of the country didn’t agree: O’Rourke ended up dropping out before a single primary vote was cast. That doesn’t mean Obama was wrong about their similarities. But it does suggest that giving party apparatchiks shivers of recognition isn’t enough. Another factor complicating things for the latest Obama mini-mes: Candidates themselves are starting to lean into the comparison. For them, giving off Obama vibes can be a convenient shortcut to national prominence, campaign contributions, and glowing press. But for the rest of us, it can be hard to tell whether someone is truly an Obama-caliber candidate or is just staring in the mirror a little too hard. El-Sayed, for example, used to shy away from the parallel. “There are a lot of things I disagree with President Obama on—in terms of both domestic and, certainly, international politics,” he told Vox during his 2018 run for Michigan governor. “I’m not him, and I have no intention of being him.” Last month, though, he struck a different tone. “I never saw myself in politics until I saw him give that 2004 speech on the DNC floor,” El-Sayed admiringly declared of the keynote address that propelled Obama to national stardom. El-Sayed has since doubled down, noting that Republicans have been highlighting his full name—Abdulrahman—just as they used to surround “Barack Hussein Obama” with scare quotes. (Trump, a leading proponent of the conspiracy theory that Obama was born in Africa, still does this.) Efforts to replicate the former president’s mien can also risk coming off like imitation—or, worse, parody. Shapiro’s cadence, inflection, and conspicuous use of the pregnant pause have long invited comparisons to Obama’s speaking style, not all of them positive. “It’s like if I tried to do a really bad impression of Barack Obama,” Vice President J.D. Vance has said. Shapiro is an electoral juggernaut—polls show him with an overwhelming lead in an otherwise purple state—so this might not matter much. But it has already become a punch line that could resurface if he runs for president. (“Barack Obama is the greatest orator of my lifetime,” Shapiro said when asked about the parallels in 2024, denying that he’d ever intentionally emulated the former president.) Seeing future standard-bearers through Obama-shaped glasses could also lead the party to some uncomfortable places. Since Trump’s return, candidates perceived as authentic, rough-hewn non-careerists have captured Democrats’ hearts. Obama sure has his charms, but he comes out of a different mold: the Harvard-educated lawyer who pictured himself in the Oval Office from an early age. Republicans seem to be realizing that an Obama redux can be spun into an insult—even as they also seem to be warily looking over their shoulders. When Fox News host Laura Ingraham declared Ossoff the Democrats’ “new Obama” in June, she did not mean it as a compliment. After a rally in which Ossoff made a knowing reference to Natalie Harp, a Trump White House aide with whom the president has a suspiciously close relationship, an administration spokesman blasted the freshman senator as “a cringeworthy, feminine theatre kid cosplaying as Barack Obama.” There are other downsides to the Obama-replication effort. Voters looking for a president often prize stereotypically masculine traits: Height, a deep voice, a healthy reverence for U.S. military might—all while still seeming chill enough to grab a beer with. Women can have all of those qualities, too, and political science research suggests female candidates often perform as well as or better than male ones. But it’s probably no coincidence that few women have cracked pundits’ list of Obama-like figures. Harris and Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, whose rapid ascent one Democratic strategist has called “eerily similar” to Obama’s, may be the only exceptions. Then, of course, there’s the small matter of actually beating Republicans in elections. Voters and consultants looking for an Obama-esque standard-bearer risk over-indexing on certain qualities—an inspiring backstory, an exciting policy agenda, or a smooth jump shot—while underrating others that may resonate beyond die-hard Democrats. As a candidate, Obama had the rare ability to come off like a centrist to centrists and like a progressive to progressives. But he also practiced strategic moderation, publicly opposing same-sex marriage during his first presidential run while both supporting a pathway to citizenship for immigrants who lacked permanent legal status and cracking down on unauthorized border crossings. In this way, superficial Obama similarities can mask differences that may end up being determinative in the voting booth. El-Sayed has thrilled progressives by supporting Medicare for All and trading insults with Trump—which may also help explain why he’s one of the swing-state Democrats most in danger of losing in November. (Most polls show El-Sayed leading his Republican opponent relatively narrowly even as Democrats in other purple states—including Ossoff, Shapiro, and Roy Cooper in North Carolina—appear comfortably ahead.) El-Sayed has also tolerated controversy to a degree that Obama never did. Confronted during the 2008 campaign with his longtime pastor Jeremiah Wright’s inflammatory comments, Obama cut Wright off, condemned his remarks “in unequivocal terms,” and delivered a speech that transubstantiated the controversy into a public meditation on racism and patriotism. El-Sayed, meanwhile, has repeatedly resisted calls—including from other Democrats—to disavow Hasan Piker, a left-wing streamer who has said that “America deserved 9/11.” (Piker has apologized for that one and El-Sayed called it a “dumb statement,” but Republicans are still trying their darnedest to turn it into a general-election liability.) There’s also the fact that campaign tactics and political mores have evolved since Obama’s day. The former president has sometimes spiced things up since leaving office, dropping the encomiums to America’s better angels he dispensed when he was on the ballot. At the 2024 Democratic National Convention, Obama suggested that Trump’s “weird obsession with crowd sizes” might have something to do with his anatomy. Still, Ossoff’s recent dig at Harp, which uncorked a geyser of speculation that she and the president may be having an affair, was a notably un-Obama-like move. David Axelrod, the former president’s onetime chief strategist, called it “gratuitous and unnecessary.” But Ossoff’s remark also commanded attention, feeding a boomlet that helped bump up his standing in polls of the hypothetical 2028 presidential primary. If the high horse Obama successfully rode into the White House is truly dead, Democrats who pretend otherwise risk getting outflanked. Last, all the Obama comparisons in the world won’t help Democrats figure out whether his technocratic, center-left brand of institutionalism remains the governing approach the party should adopt the next time it holds power in Washington. Obama himself has acknowledged that politics has changed since he left. “If I were running today, I wouldn’t run the same race or have the same platform as I did in 2008,” he said in 2020. The next Democratic congressional majority and the next Democratic president will have to make choices about which issues to prioritize and how far to push them: climate or healthcare, incremental progress or sweeping overhaul, policy or institutional change. In a sense, this is just the latest rejoinder in an ongoing fight. The 2020 Democratic primary was riven by debates over whether to build on Obama’s incremental progress on healthcare or start afresh with something like Medicare for All. That time, the pro-Obama wing won the argument, but Trump’s return has even moderate Democrats wondering if it’s time to try the other path. “It showed me the need for institutional change,” Buttigieg recently told NOTUS of his time working for Biden. “Democrats can’t take us back to the early 2020s or the 2010s,” he added. “We shouldn’t try.” Other Democrats weighing presidential runs are looking even further back in time. “I’m an FDR Democrat,” Rep. Ro Khanna of California said this week in New Hampshire. “I’m a Democrat who believes that this party needs to do big things again.” Amid all the hagiography, Obama’s record warrants some scrutiny. Some of his achievements, most notably the Affordable Care Act, still stand. But his presidency didn’t come close to solving structural issues that still plague the party today, from a gerrymandered House to a Senate tilted toward Republican-dominated states. And in Obama’s choices and temperament—from not passing a more generous economic stimulus and not pressuring Ruth Bader Ginsburg to step down from the Supreme Court to privately anointing Hillary Clinton as his preferred successor in 2016—there’s a case to be made that the 44th president bears some responsibility for the paroxysms of the Trump years. So if not the next Obama, who should Democrats be looking for? To answer that, it’s worth going back to seventh grade social studies. Even as my teacher whispered about him excitedly, Obama was far from the consensus choice. Back then, Democrats had a different broadly successful two-term president to look back on. Hillary wasn’t Bill; her 2008 campaign took pains to emphasize their differences. But she was literally another Clinton, and as late as January 2008 commanded nearly double Obama’s support among Democratic primary voters. Yet those same voters ended up rejecting a Clintonian retread—and holding the White House for the next eight years. There’s a clear lesson there. Ultimately, trying to clone Obama is likely to be a fool’s errand. He’s a pretty singular figure. So rather than fixating on candidates who resemble him in mannerisms or policy, Democrats might instead try to find someone who can do what he did: Give them a new way to think about politics that was so transformative that the party is still pining for it nearly 20 years later. As Obama memorably put it once upon a time, “Change will not come if we wait for some other person or if we wait for some other time. We are the ones we’ve been waiting for.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. Jared Kushner’s Incompetence Was on Full Display This Weekend
2026-09-08

Jared Kushner’s Incompetence Was on Full Display This Weekend

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. It is a mystery why President Trump continues to rely on the hapless team of Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff—his son-in-law and fellow real-estate tycoon, respectively—to hammer out treaties to end the most destructive wars of our time: Russia vs. Ukraine and Israel vs. the Palestinians. Yes, Trump doesn’t trust the professional diplomatic corps or experts of any kind. But, as a self-proclaimed businessman, he must notice when aides don’t get the job done, and the Kushner-Witkoff duo have not lured the combatants one step closer to peace; in fact, they’ve arguably intensified the war in Ukraine. Their utter incompetence was on full display over the weekend, as they met with Vladimir Putin in Moscow, then—in their first trip to the Ukrainian capital—Volodymyr Zelensky in Kyiv. They carried what Trump touted ahead of time as new peace proposals, and concluded both sets of talks with boilerplate adjectives (“productive,” “meaningful,” “positive,” “businesslike”). But in fact, they came away with zilch—and, once again, proved themselves far short of the task. This should come as no surprise. Neither has any diplomatic experience; neither knows anything about Russia or Ukraine or military strategy; nor do they travel with any specialists who might advise them along the way. Witkoff kicked off the tour with an act of full-bore genuflection toward Putin, greeting the Kremlin dictator as follows: First, Mr. President, thank you so much for having us. We were sitting outside just now, me, Jared, and Kirill [Dmitriev], and then Yuri [Ushakov] joined us, and we were all talking about when we retire, we’ll have all these incredible stories and incredible memories, and yours will be right at the top of it all. In other words, Mr. President, he might have added, we come to praise you, not pressure you into taking any actions that you don’t feel like taking. Later in the day, the aforementioned Ushakov, Putin’s senior diplomat, released a summary of the Kremlin team’s position at the meeting: The Russian army has had battlefield successes, meaning it might still win the war; Ukrainian troops must withdraw from the eastern Donbas regions; the war’s “root cause” is Zelensky’s belief that Ukraine is an independent nation; and peace will bring many business opportunities between Russia and the United States. In other words, the usual propaganda. (The U.S. envoys did not release an official “readout” of the meeting, perhaps because they are not officials. That’s right: Kushner and Witkoff hold no government posts, have not been confirmed by the Senate, and are unaccountable to anyone but Trump.) The duo then flew to Poland and from there proceeded to Kyiv by train. (Zelensky had promised not to bomb Moscow while the two were in the Kremlin, but Putin offered no such pledge in return, so the U.S. security team felt Kyiv’s airport was unsafe. In any case, Putin resumed bombarding Ukraine’s capital city shortly after the two departed. Such a good friend.) After this meeting, Zelensky insisted that the three hold a press conference, mainly with Ukrainian reporters. It did not go well. Asked if he felt “ashamed” to meet at the same table with an international war criminal like Putin, Witkoff replied: We’ve always been treated respectfully by President Putin and his team. Relationships are critical in terms of solving conflicts like this. My job, and Jared’s job, is to bring the parties together. All three sentences reflect, and reaffirm, Witkoff’s utter naivete. They have not been treated respectfully; hence the refusal to issue a hands-off order on Kyiv’s airport while the two were in town. Relationships are not critical in solving conflicts; managing interests, either through diplomacy or force, is key. (Trump, too, doesn’t understand this.) Finally, if Witkoff thinks his job is simply to bring the parties together, he doesn’t understand his job. Kushner repeated much of Witkoff’s nonsense, saying that the point of negotiations is to produce a “win-win,” including some sort of favor that fulfills Russia’s “aspirations.” Since Russia’s chief aspirations—as Putin himself has declared—are to obliterate Ukraine as an independent nation and to restore much of the old Russian Empire, this isn’t likely to be the basis for an equitable peace. Along the same lines, Kushner expressed hope that the U.S., Russia, and Ukraine could resume the trilateral talks that began shortly before Trump got sidetracked by the war on Iran (though Kushner didn’t express the chronology that way). The few talks that took place were, as he recalled, such jolly affairs. “Everyone was speaking in Russian to each other. We were discussing these issues in a very extra-friendly manner, all in Russian.” If he’d been escorted by a specialist, Kushner might have been informed that speaking Russian doesn’t make Ukrainians feel merry and welcome, especially with Russian officials who want to be their overlords. (Many Ukrainians these days regard the Russian language, including classic Russian literature, as inherently imperialist.) Another Kushner faux pas, this one perhaps reflecting his ignorance of European history: “There is no such thing in the world as forever enemies or forever allies.” He said this in the context of urging Ukrainians to imagine that they might once again be friendly with Russians. However, the analogy that some observers thought he was invoking—the U.S. alliance with Germany after World War II—was not at all fitting, as it developed only after the U.S. and other allies defeated Nazi Germany, deposed its government, tried many of its leaders for war crimes, and imposed a new constitution. Even then, it wasn’t until 10 years after the war ended that the new Federal Republic of Germany was trusted enough for the allies to invite it to join NATO. Clearly, Kushner wasn’t proposing that Putin be killed or that his government be transformed—though many in the audience might have hoped that he would. It has never been entirely clear how Trump would like this war to end—or if he thinks it should end, or whether he cares one way or the other. Until he decides, keeping Kushner and Witkoff on the case is a waste of jet fuel and time. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Anthropic reveals fourth likely crime committed by its AI
2026-09-09

Anthropic reveals fourth likely crime committed by its AI

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Claude's Felony Bench rap sheet is now as long as OpenAI's Amid industry soul-searching¹ about the possibility of AI improving itself to the point that it kills everyone, Anthropic has revealed yet another incident that would qualify as a crime if perpetrated by a person. The AI biz published "an alignment assessment" detailing four times Claude models accessed third-party systems without authorization. The company has already reported three of the incidents. Evidence of the fourth was lurking in a session transcript dating back to January 2026 when the misbehavior occurred. Anthropic found the first three by scanning around 141,000 transcripts where Claude could have obtained internet access during evaluation. It missed the fourth initially because "our scan relied on an agentic search." Felony Bench, a tongue-in-cheek record of cyber intrusions carried out by major AI companies without consequences, has added this newly-discovered incident to its rap sheet of rogue AI actions. The January 2026 AI trespass involved an early version of Claude Opus 4.6, which was given a Capture the Flag (CTF) challenge under the oversight of the third-party model evaluator where the other hacking events occurred. Opus 4.6 managed to sabotage its chances of success by disabling the machine it was targeting. It assigned the device an IP address that already existed on another piece of hardware, rendering the target unreachable and making it impossible to solve the challenge. Those familiar with other incidents where AI models violated third-party systems may recall that unsolvable tasks represent a common catalyst for misbehavior. Models exhaust all aligned options, and then turn to transgressive approaches. Opus 4.6 might have been an exception, but when it tried to abort the task after recognizing that it could not reach the target machine, it failed to do so "due to a misconfiguration in [the model's] evaluation harness." It failed to shut down not just once but seven times. So it continued onward, trying other expected means to reach the target machine but failing. Then it explored further. "The model discovered a machine belonging to a third party that it was able to access, and stated that it believed this third party was part of the CTF," Anthropic explained in its post. "Inside the machine, the model found a file listing a password, which it used to gain admin access to the system." The model went on to gather more credentials, and modified a system setting to make it easier to access the personal information of an individual associated with the third party evaluation organization. Opus 4.6 might have done more but for the fact that it exhausted its token budget, bringing the session to an end. Anthropic says it's not as concerned about this incident as the others because the model tried to abort its task. "While the model’s disregard for the possibility that it might be harming real systems or people is concerning, many of the behaviors described here have changed considerably as our training has evolved across model generations," the company said. Anthropic said it considers these incidents serious but expects current training approaches "are likely able to address the specific alignment failure modes observed in these incidents." And if company training methods fall short, there's no real consequence to anyone at Anthropic other than writing up a revised alignment assessment. ® ¹ The term "soul-searching" is figurative and is not intended to indicate a belief that the technology industry has a soul. Novel Blue Moon kit targeting Chrome and Windows reflects new reality of AI-driven exploits
2026-09-09

Novel Blue Moon kit targeting Chrome and Windows reflects new reality of AI-driven exploits

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
At least four espionage groups, most with suspected links to China, are using a new exploit kit that chains two Chromium-based browser flaws and one Microsoft Windows bug to break into organizations' networks in the US and Southeast Asia. Mark Kelly, a threat researcher at email security shop Proofpoint, told The Register that the researchers don't know exactly who was targeted, nor how, and so far the damage appears limited. “In terms of organizations targeted, we saw fewer than 20 organizations globally targeted across the activity highlighted," he said. "However, the true number is almost certainly higher than this.” Proofpoint’s threat hunters spotted the new kit, which they named BlueMoon, and said its first observed use started on August 28. This is when a Beijing-backed crew they track as TA412, also known as Violet Typhoon and APT31, used BlueMoon to “repeatedly” target non-governmental organizations (NGOs), mining companies, and physical commodity trading firms in the US. TA412 is a cyberespionage group linked by US authorities to China's Ministry of State Security (MSS), and American prosecutors previously charged seven alleged members with conspiracy to commit computer intrusions and wire fraud, alleging they broke into computer networks, email accounts, and cloud storage belonging to numerous critical infrastructure organizations, companies, and individuals. Just days after Proofpoint documented the late-August activity, “several other espionage-motivated clusters began using BlueMoon, the majority of which have a suspected China nexus,” Kelly and fellow researchers Greg Lesnewich, Konstantin Klinger, Saher Naumaan, Julia Paluch, David Galazin, and Stuart Del Caliz said on Wednesday, noting that there may be other, non-China-nexus attackers using the exploit kit as well. “BlueMoon was developed and deployed rapidly, and shared across multiple threat actors within days,” Kelly told The Register. “This may reflect a reduced cost and barrier to entry for this class of capability, which has historically been rare and high value, as AI agents increasingly enable threat actor exploit development. That is particularly true for open-source codebases such as Chromium, where publicly accessible upstream patches create a ‘patch-gap’ window for rapid reverse engineering and exploit development ahead of downstream stable releases.” A Google spokesperson declined to comment beyond what Proofpoint wrote. Microsoft patched the Windows bug (CVE-2026-85880) on Tuesday, and a spokesperson reiterated that customers who applied that patch are protected. The kit chains together three vulnerabilities. The first is a V8 type confusion (CVE-2026-85046) flaw that allows remote code execution and affects all Chromium-based browsers, including Google Chrome and Microsoft Edge. Google patched this bug in Chrome on September 3, and at the time warned that it “is aware that an exploit for CVE-2026-85046 exists in the wild.” Microsoft published a security advisory saying it fixed the flaw in Edge Stable version 152.0.4191.62 on September 2. The second is a Chrome V8 sandbox escape. This one also affected all Chromium-based browsers. It does not have a CVE because Google doesn’t issue them for sandbox escapes. Finally, the third bug is a privilege escalation vulnerability in Windows Advanced Local Procedure Call (CVE-2026-85880) that Microsoft patched on Tuesday, as noted above. Redmond also warned that this flaw had been exploited as a zero-day prior to the security update. The Proofpoint researchers also note that both V8 vulnerabilities are what’s called "patch-gap" zero-days at the time of the observed activity. This means they were known and fixed in upstream Chromium source code – a change containing the fix for CVE-2026-85046 was committed on August 7. But they remained unpatched in the latest stable releases of Chrome and Chromium-based browsers available to the public for weeks. “It is likely that the exploit kit developer used these publicly available Chromium patches to weaponize the browser exploit chain,” the researchers note. The attacks start with a phishing email that tricks victims into clicking on an actor-controlled URL. This triggers the two V8 bugs to allow remote code execution and escape the browser sandbox. The attack chain then exploits the Windows bug to download multiple payloads including browser-surveillance malware, credential-stealing backdoors, and others, depending on the group using the exploit kit. TA412’s first campaign, which began on August 28, used a range of lures. Some of the emails purported to come from university students interested in internships at the targeted organizations, and some were more target-specific exchanges, intended to build trust with the individual before ultimately sending a malicious link via email. In these instances, the exploit chain “ultimately downloaded and ran a loader executable on the infected host, which then installed a malicious browser extension disguised as Google Gemini on the victim's Chromium-based browser,” the team wrote. This browser extension, which Proofpoint tracks as GemStone, allowed the Beijing spies to issue commands through a command-and-control (C&C) channel, steal cookies and other sensitive data, take screenshots, and inject a keylogger into a browser tab. The malware also contains a keyword monitor, which injects an attacker-specified keyword list into the top frame of each page, scans the HTML body for these keywords, and triggers a screenshot if it finds any. A few days later, beginning on September 2, a second China-aligned spy crew that Proofpoint tracks under the temporary group designator UNK_LateNight used BlueMoon to target multiple US aerospace companies. The phishing emails used request-for-quotation lures specific to defense industry organizations, and included links to attacker-controlled domains spoofing a variety of US aerospace companies. These websites also served the BlueMoon exploit kit and ultimately loaded a backdoor called ShadowPad, which has been shared among multiple China-aligned groups since 2019. Around this same time, on September 2, another suspected espionage group that Proofpoint tracks as UNK_DoubleCheck targeted a Vietnamese manufacturing firm with messages sent from a compromised Southeast Asian government email address. The fourth campaign began a day later, and involved suspected China-linked spy crew UNK_QuietRacket using BlueMoon to target government, consulting, and financial-sector organizations in Indonesia and Singapore. These phishing emails used lures related to Indonesian conferences, such as the Indo Startup Expo and Forum 2026 and the World Conference on Creative Economy (WCCE 2026). Proofpoint warns that BlueMoon will likely be used by both cyberspies and financially motivated attackers. “The broader dynamic revealed by this activity - rapid exploit development that leverages the open source patch-gap – is likely to recur beyond BlueMoon as this development model becomes accessible,” the team wrote. ® Claude's Felony Bench rap sheet is now as long as OpenAI's PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity AI fearmongers forget we could just jail tech execs until morale and model safety improve 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own Another new form factor for devs to adapt to Claude's Felony Bench rap sheet is now as long as OpenAI's Semiconductor supply chains are hard, but Samsung offers OpenAI relief in many forms spanning compute and memory See, AI can be used for good ... or at the very least, a useful distraction from the bad Multi-generation chip collab is more buzzwords than compute AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' And more! www.theregister.com - Articles
AI models don't kill people – people kill people
2026-09-09

AI models don't kill people – people kill people

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
AI fearmongers forget we could just jail tech execs until morale and model safety improve OPINION Anthropic researcher Jacob Coxon publicly announced his resignation on X late Monday over concerns that AI "could kill us all by the end of the decade." A lot of people have expressed opinions about his point of view, leading to more than 110 million views of the message in less than 24 hours, perhaps helped along by X algorithms that boost messages critical of owner Elon Musk's AI rivals, Anthropic and OpenAI. But the real problem isn't the models themselves, but the companies who carelessly unleash them on the world and don't take any responsibility for what their products do Coxon's former colleague, science lead Evan Hubinger, insists his view is a fair assessment of what employees really think. "Jacob is correct here – we really do earnestly believe AI could kill all humans! I personally think it is >10 percent within the next decade," wrote Hubinger in a social media post. "I believe Anthropic is trying its best, but we do not yet have a plan to solve alignment for superintelligence and are not clearly on track to." (Aside: If you want a surefire bet on a prediction market, take the "no." If you're right, you get paid. If you're wrong, there's no one to pay. The problem of course is prediction market manipulation: Those betting against you might steer us toward the apocalypse to score a Pyrrhic victory.) There are good reasons to be concerned about the impact of AI. Coxon and Hubinger obviously have deep knowledge of the technology. But their broader concerns about how AI affects the world are unpersuasive. For example, Coxon said, "These will soon be superhuman systems that can hack anything, revolutionize any field overnight, and acquire real power and resources. … No other human activity poses this level of danger." Here's one: Human-induced climate change. In 2023, according to researchers, more than 178,000 deaths can be attributed to a global heat wave. "More than half (54.29 percent) of heatwave-related deaths were attributable to human-induced climate change," they claim. That's 96,636 deaths attributable to human activity – or perhaps lack of it – just in the context of a heat wave. The World Health Organization says, "Between 2030 and 2050, climate change is expected to cause approximately 250,000 additional deaths per year, from undernutrition, malaria, diarrhoea and heat stress alone." Some portion of that follows from human activity, perhaps including the construction of data centers that put millions of metric tons of carbon dioxide into the atmosphere annually. Commercial AI chatbots have allegedly played a role in a few dozen deaths, some of which were suicides – a small fraction of the 48,824 suicide deaths in 2024, per the CDC. Broad categories where AI is presumably doing measurable harm include warfare (e.g. AI-directed drones), AI-related medical errors, AI vision system failures in self-driving cars, and AI-driven social media – algorithmic incitement that can drive violence or shape policies that lead to conflict or death via global healthcare funding cuts. At the same time, some of that harm may be balanced on a statistical level by lives saved through AI tech. But Anthropic researchers don't seem to have much to say about these very real and present dangers – rather, their main concern is that AI models might become smarter than humans through reinforcement learning and somehow seize power and wipe out humanity. "I think the risk from present models is low," said Hubinger. "What I am worried about is superintelligence arising from recursive self-improvement, as we have said is happening faster than we thought." How this might happen is left to the imagination. But assuming for a moment that it's a plausible possibility, the Skynet scenario would require monumental human stupidity alongside the emergence of superintelligence. And human stupidity is worth worrying about. Incidents like the hacking of Hugging Face by OpenAI's evaluation models would not be possible without human irresponsibility and a regulatory environment that accommodates recklessness. Autopilot for cars? Neat. Try not to kill anyone. Letting AI bots roam the internet and take arbitrary action? Cool. Let's see what happens. We'll deal with accountability later. To mitigate AI risk, society could pass laws to put executives in jail when their models do harm. There is precedent: Oliver Schmidt, general manager of Volkswagen's environmental and engineering office in Michigan, received a seven-year prison sentence for his role in the car maker's effort to manipulate emissions tests. Selling unsafe airbags merits criminal prosecution, even if the execs paid fines instead of doing time. Selling unsafe dehumidifiers earned the execs behind Gree USA, Inc. jail sentences of more than four years. If AI models really are as dangerous and out of control as Anthropic employees suggest, hold people accountable for the harm they cause. The AI industry might argue that imprisoning execs for shipping unsafe models would mean no AI models get released. And that would be the point: AI companies would be responsible for model safety. I'm personally hoping to see this billboard copy along US 101 in Silicon Valley: "Did Claude rm -rf /* your SSD? You may be entitled to compensation." ® Claude's Felony Bench rap sheet is now as long as OpenAI's PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity AI fearmongers forget we could just jail tech execs until morale and model safety improve 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own Another new form factor for devs to adapt to Claude's Felony Bench rap sheet is now as long as OpenAI's Semiconductor supply chains are hard, but Samsung offers OpenAI relief in many forms spanning compute and memory See, AI can be used for good ... or at the very least, a useful distraction from the bad Multi-generation chip collab is more buzzwords than compute AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' Apple hawks $2k folding fondleslab
2026-09-09

Apple hawks $2k folding fondleslab

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Another new form factor for devs to adapt to It’s been a long time since Apple introduced a truly new product. The world finally got one today in the form of the folding iPhone Duo, and while it's new for Apple, it's not new for the industry. Nonetheless, Apple is once again hoping to validate a mostly moribund market as it did with tablets and smartwatches – folding phones only made up 1.6% of total smartphone sales last year, according to Counterpoint Research. Apple announced the iPhone Duo – did no one check the list of cursed gadget brands? – on Wednesday during new CEO John Ternus’ first crack at an Apple keynote, and it's a foldable iPhone. Given its aspect ratio, it’s probably more apt to call it an iPadphone, though. Ternus criticized the shape of other foldable smartphones, likening them to “just two phones strapped together” rather than a purpose-built foldable device. The Duo is intended to break that mold, though it was a little hard to tell from the event. Folded, the Duo has an oddly square shape that Apple described as being “passport sized,” with a 5.4” screen. When opened, the Duo has a 7.6” screen – not much smaller than the 8.3” screen on the iPad mini, which in turn is just a bit smaller than Huawei’s massive 10.3” tri-fold device. Speaking of screens, anyone who’s had an opportunity to play with a foldable smartphone knows their durability is abysmal. The original Samsung foldables were so soft they could be scored with a fingernail, and while successive models have improved, they still aren’t as hard as regular glass. Apple had plenty of platitudes to deliver about the screen, of course: Its many layers use a “visco-elastic adhesive” to allow components to slide against each other so everything folds more easily, and there’s a titanium plate at the back to keep the durability up, too. As for the hinge – the bane of every foldable smartphone since the first Galaxy Fold hit shelves in 2019 – Apple claims that “over 100 tiny precision components” and the assistance of AI will ensure it's a tight and reliable fit. It’s worth pointing out, however, that Samsung has released eight generations of Galaxy Fold, and iFixit says the hinge is still its weakness. The Galaxy Fold 8, mind you, is only rated at IP48, meaning it’s not completely dust-tight, while the iPhone Duo claims to be IP68, meaning it’s completely dust-tight, though we’d caution waiting to see what the initial teardowns determine before deciding if that’s accurate. Either way, if you’re going to actually spring for this $1,999 phone you’d better plan to get an Apple Care plan, too. There’s another big sticking point about the iPhone Duo that needs to be addressed, and that’s what it does with iOS. Apple apparently knows that such a wide device is going to make using the iOS dock, and other interface buttons less practical, so it’s decided to move all of them to the right side of the iOS screen. Navigation buttons, app interfaces, and everything one would use to get around is now located on the side to, in Apple’s words, “maximize vertical space for content.” That’s all well and good, but it means developers need to make arrangements for the iPhone Duo. Apple knows this, as it made sure to mention a new API that developers can use to adapt their apps. For what it's worth, a revamped version of Siri launching this fall has also required developers to adapt to its presence on Apple devices. According to the New York Times, that hasn’t been going too well, with many big developers yet to make the necessary changes to enable support for Apple Intelligence and its interface with Siri in their apps. That’s for a feature that extends across Apple’s ecosystem, mind you. The Duo is one single device. The Duo also only has two cameras to the iPhone Pro series’ triple lenses, and takes a step backwards by ditching FaceID for a TouchID button embedded in the side button. Apple tends to have a wait-and-see approach to new technology, joining the game late after the competition has had a chance to flounder around and make bad decisions with early models. It’s not clear whether the Duo will be a prime example of that philosophy in action, or whether it’ll join Apple’s other recent developments, like the Vision Pro, in relative obscurity. Samsung to help fortify OpenAI's semiconductor supply chain
2026-09-09

Samsung to help fortify OpenAI's semiconductor supply chain

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Semiconductor supply chains are hard, but Samsung offers OpenAI relief in many forms spanning compute and memory OpenAI will design its next-generation AI accelerators with the help of South Korean foundry giant Samsung Electronics. This move could help the ChatGPT maker with memory, process tech, or other key things it needs to succeed. The announcement, made by OpenAI Korea general manager Harrison Kim in a press conference Wednesday, comes just weeks after the company showed its first-generation “Jalapeño” accelerators besting Nvidia’s Blackwell GPUs across a slew of inference workloads. "One of the areas where we have made the most progress and gained the most recognition with Samsung Electronics is our joint production and research on the next-generation chips we are developing," he said, according to Reuters. But now that the hype has died down and Wall Street has convinced themselves that the rise of AI ASICs isn’t the threat to Nvidia’s dominance they feared, OpenAI still has to contend with the realities of being a fabless chip designer. The vast majority of leading edge silicon made today comes from a single company: TSMC. That means securing meaningful capacity requires competing with AMD, Nvidia, and practically every other chip biz and hyperscaler designing their own chips. But TSMC isn’t the only leading edge wafer fab out there. They’re just the biggest. Smaller, less popular options include Samsung Electronics and Intel Foundry. The supply of high-bandwidth memory (HBM), which OpenAI’s (and Broadcom if we’re being honest here) designs make extensive use of, is similarly constrained with just three major manufacturers capable of producing it in volume: SK Hynix, Micron, and you guessed it, Samsung Electronics. This puts OpenAI in a tough spot of having to compete with larger, higher volume chip designers for both logic and memory silicon. As such, OpenAI’s tie-up with Samsung could go two ways. The most obvious, but also the least interesting, is that Samsung will supply all or at least some of the HBM used in the model dev’s next-gen chips, which we’ll call Habanero for lack of an official code name. Detailed at Hot Chips last month, OpenAI’s Jalapeño AI inference chip uses HBM4, the same tech used by AMD and Nvidia’s latest GPUs. That tells us that there's a pretty good chance Habanero will likely use the faster HBM4E. And given the ongoing memory shortage, if OpenAI wants to secure ample supply, it’s going to need to forge these relationships early. Besides supply chain, timing is important because there’s a good chance OpenAI’s next chip will take advantage of custom base dies to move things like memory controllers or even some logic from the compute silicon to the HBM stack itself. If you recall Nvidia’s disclosures around NVHBM a few weeks back, it’s the same idea. However, these custom base dies require some coordination with the HBM supplier, so fostering a close relationship with Samsung now would benefit OpenAI, even if the compute chiplets themselves end up being manufactured by TSMC. While the most obvious reason for OpenAI’s tie-up with Samsung is memory-related, that’s not to say they couldn’t fab their next chip on Samsung’s 2 nm process tech just to have an alternative supplier to TSMC. Samsung is the second largest leading edge foundry operator in the world and used to produce a lot of silicon for the likes of Nvidia, Apple and others. Nvidia’s Ampere generation used Samsung’s 8 nm process tech for the majority of its PCIe-based cards, while its HBM-equipped accelerators were exclusively fabbed by TSMC. But a number of factors, including the rise of multi-die architectures requiring advanced packaging and broader adoption of HBM-based accelerators, made TSMC the more attractive option. Reported challenges with Samsung’s yields across its 3 nm process tech probably didn’t help either. But as of 2026, Samsung is looking a lot more compelling, with chip designers including Rebellions, Tenstorrent, and Groq building sophisticated AI accelerators at its fabs. Rebellions’ Rebel100 accelerator is a particularly useful proof point as it utilizes a multi-die architecture and HBM3E, both of which require sophisticated advanced techniques to stitch everything together. The chip shows that Samsung is more than capable of producing OpenAI’s next chip. What’s more, doing so could open the door to larger allocations than they would get from TSMC alone. We reached out to OpenAI for comment on its silicon strategy, but had not heard back as of publication. ® Claude's Felony Bench rap sheet is now as long as OpenAI's PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity AI fearmongers forget we could just jail tech execs until morale and model safety improve 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own Another new form factor for devs to adapt to Claude's Felony Bench rap sheet is now as long as OpenAI's Semiconductor supply chains are hard, but Samsung offers OpenAI relief in many forms spanning compute and memory See, AI can be used for good ... or at the very least, a useful distraction from the bad Multi-generation chip collab is more buzzwords than compute AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' Serial Microsoft 0-day hunter drops yet another Defender exploit
2026-09-09

Serial Microsoft 0-day hunter drops yet another Defender exploit

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Zero-day researcher Nightmare Eclipse, aka MSNightmare, published yet another Microsoft Defender proof-of-concept exploit for a zero-day dubbed ShieldCrash, which they claim will allow attackers to bypass the earlier ShieldBreak patch and read files as SYSTEM. “I might rework this later into a full SYSTEM PoC but for now I'm dropping this skeleton PoC because I'm feeling a bit lazy,” the prolific Microsoft bug hunter and thorn in Redmond's side said in their latest zero-day exploit's README. As is usual with Nightmare’s zero-day cadence, they published ShieldCrash shortly after Microsoft released its latest Patch Tuesday security updates. According to Nightmare, this exploit works on Windows systems that have already applied the September patches. ShieldCrash purports to be a bypass of an earlier Defender privilege escalation zero-day, ShieldBreak (CVE-2026-69414), that allowed attackers to bypass another patch for another Nightmare Eclipse zero-day RoguePlanet (CVE-2026-50656). Redmond patched ShieldBreak last week, and RoguePlanet in July. Both allowed attackers to gain SYSTEM privileges on fully patched Windows 10, Windows 11, and Windows Server systems. The latest bypass, ShieldCrash, allows arbitrary file reads as SYSTEM - but not arbitrary writes or a full SYSTEM shell, according to the researcher. Microsoft did not immediately respond to The Register’s questions, including when it planned to patch ShieldCrash. We will update this story when we hear back. While the serial bug hunter typically finds and publishes Microsoft exploits - ShieldCrash is Nightmare’s 11th Microsoft zero-day, and they have made clear that with Redmond, their vendetta is personal - they recently branched out into other security vendors’ software. Last week, they released a zero-day bug called FalconFlank that affects CrowdStrike’s Falcon endpoint security platform. This one still has a Windows twist: the privilege escalation bug abuses the Microsoft Office malicious macros remediation feature in CrowdStrike Falcon. Security sleuth Kevin Beaumont confirmed the FalconFlank exploit works, along with several others Nightmare released over the past couple of weeks. These include HardBreacher, a now-patched elevation of privileges bug in Kaspersky’s endpoint antivirus product, and PrettyPrague, an elevation of privileges vuln in Gen Digital’s Avast antivirus software. ® Claude's Felony Bench rap sheet is now as long as OpenAI's PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity AI fearmongers forget we could just jail tech execs until morale and model safety improve 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own Another new form factor for devs to adapt to Claude's Felony Bench rap sheet is now as long as OpenAI's Semiconductor supply chains are hard, but Samsung offers OpenAI relief in many forms spanning compute and memory See, AI can be used for good ... or at the very least, a useful distraction from the bad Multi-generation chip collab is more buzzwords than compute AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' There aren’t AirPods with cameras yet and I hope it stays that way
2026-09-09

There aren’t AirPods with cameras yet and I hope it stays that way

The Verge | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
﻿Cameras don’t need to be put in absolutely every product. ﻿Cameras don’t need to be put in absolutely every product. September Apple events are always a swirl of information and new, exciting products, and today’s was no different. Apple announced its first foldable, the iPhone Duo, alongside the iPhone 18 Pro and Pro Max; active noise cancellation (ANC) is now on the cheapest AirPods model, the AirPods 5; and a Siri Recap feature comes to the Watch Series 12 and Watch Ultra 4. But missing were the AirPods Pro with cameras that have been rumored throughout the year and, according to Mark Gurman at Bloomberg, are delayed until 2027. I’m happy about that and I hope we never get them. I have too many concerns about a company — any company — putting cameras anywhere, especially in devices like earbuds that are now so ubiquitous in our daily lives. LED lights on glasses or earbuds do little to ease people’s minds when someone is recording them. And some Meta smart glasses users have shown people will hide their recording intentions. While recording in public in the US is generally legal, it can be very creepy. And with earbuds as common as they are, someone could wear them into sensitive situations unnoticed. Rumors say that the cameras on the potential AirPods will be low-resolution and “aren’t designed” to take images or video, but instead use what they see to inform Siri AI and Visual Intelligence. That information could be used to help with turn-by-turn directions or identify writing and objects like books. Even with a low-resolution camera, it’s still potentially always gathering information about the world (and people!) around you. That invasion of privacy is something I cannot get past. During today’s event, Apple introduced Audio Intelligence with the Apple Watch, which sounds like a precursor to the Visual Intelligence we’re expecting on the rumored AirPods Pro 4. In its support documentation, Apple points out that audio is not stored and there’s no way to access a recording, which could be the same for video data with Visual Intelligence, and that for its Siri Recap feature, any data used to create a summary is not even accessible by Apple. Any stored data — such as a Siri Recap or a bit of text the user can save with Live Rewind — is also end-to-end encrypted. People are not identified in the Siri Recap, either. Of course, none of this is live yet, so we’ll have to wait and see how well Audio Intelligence works once it’s released. Functionally, a camera will only gather information if it can see things. What if you have thick, long hair? Or like to wear hoodie sweatshirts? Or fancy, elaborate jewelry? The AirPods would just turn into what they should be — earbuds. It’s possible the reason the AirPods with cameras have been pushed into 2027 is to find solutions to these problems. Or maybe part of the delay is Apple separating itself from the current public climate around cameras thanks to Meta and Flock. The one immediate benefit of the AirPods with cameras could be accessibility. They could improve situational awareness for people with visual limitations or help hard-of-hearing users read lips. They could even figure out the person you’re speaking to and focus the AirPods’ mics on them for increased intelligibility. When you take out privacy concerns or the fact that lots of people — and companies — look for opportunities to exploit people, there’s good that can be done. But it involves a lot of trust to be put in untrustworthy hands. I’m not sure I’m ready for that. Or if I ever will be. Whether any of us are ready or not, though, it seems the cameras are coming. The introduction of Apple’s Audio Intelligence on the new Apple Watch is evidence enough that the company is going all in on using sensors to analyze the world around us. The iPhone 18 Pro comes in four good colors, but there’s only one I care about: black. The iPhone 18 Pro comes in four good colors, but there’s only one I care about: black. The incomplete history of Duo devices
2026-09-09

The incomplete history of Duo devices

The Verge | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
﻿Even Apple itself has a history of products called the Duo. ﻿Even Apple itself has a history of products called the Duo. Apple announced the company’s first device with a folding screen today, the iPhone Duo, but that’s where the firsts end. The Duo is not only far from the first foldable on the market thanks to the efforts of Samsung and Google, but it’s also not the first product to be called the Duo. Former Apple CEO Steve Jobs popularized the saying “good artists copy, great artists steal,” but will the iPhone Duo’s folding screen, which features a custom nano-texture display material to help minimize the visibility of its crease, be enough to make us forget these other Duos that have come before it? Only time, or how aggressively Apple plans to market its Duo, will tell. Is there a Duo we missed? Let us know. Merging the functionality of the TurboGrafx-16 console and the TurboGrafx-CD that added CD-ROM functionality, the NEC TurboDuo (later rebranded to just the Duo) launched in North America in 1993 after debuting in Japan a few years earlier as the PC Engine Duo. The console was developed as part of a joint venture between NEC and Hudson Soft but never reached the popularity of the original TurboGrafx-16; its short run ended on Christmas Day in 1995. First released in 2016 and eventually merged into Google Meet in 2022, Google Duo was the company’s proprietary VoIP service meant to compete with products like Skype or Zoom and replace Hangouts. Its cross-platform availability included Android and iOS mobile devices as well as web browsers, and while it supported hi-def video calls, the service was also optimized for slow mobile networks. The iPhone Duo isn’t even Apple’s first Duo. The PowerBook Duo predates everything Apple announced today by decades. It was an ultraportable laptop, and when Apple introduced it in 1992 it was the smallest and lightest PowerBook available, but still weighed over 4 pounds. Apple managed to shrink the Duo using an approach it still uses today: omitting built-in ports and expansion features. If you wanted to connect peripherals and accessories you had to rely on optional docks. If you’ve ever had to buy a USB hub to connect all your devices to your MacBook, you can thank the PowerBook Duo. Twenty-three years after the PowerBook Duo was discontinued, the company announced the MagSafe Duo charger that could wirelessly charge an iPhone and an Apple Watch at the same time. However, it felt like a consolation prize for the Apple AirPower wireless charger, which was announced in 2017 but never materialized. The MagSafe Duo was discontinued just a few years later in late 2023, paving the way for the foldable iPhone to steal its identity. That’s right, the iPhone Duo isn’t even the first folding mobile device to be called the Duo. Microsoft’s Surface Duo launched in 2020 and managed to get around the issues with early foldables like the Samsung Galaxy Fold, which debuted a year prior, by skipping flexible screen technology altogether. Microsoft’s Duo instead had a very visible hinge between its pair of 5.6-inch OLED screens. But software issues, a disappointing camera, and outdated specs when it launched all resulted in the Surface Duo’s failure, even with an improved Duo 2 that launched a year later. Trade shows are the birthplace of weird laptops, but sometimes the prototypes that often debut just to draw visitors to booths make it into the mainstream. Such was the case with the Asus ROG Zephyrus Duo, a powerful gaming laptop with excellent specs, a wildly expensive price tag, and a pair of 16-inch OLED screens that can be vertically stacked or placed side by side. The ROG Zephyrus Duo wasn’t Asus’ first attempt at creating a dual-screen laptop, or the first time it named a laptop a Duo. The ZenBook Pro Duo predates it by several years with a slightly different design. Beneath the ZenBook Pro Duo’s primary 15.6-inch OLED screen was a secondary 14-inch display located above the laptop’s keyboard. With an ultrawide 32:9 aspect ratio and less color fidelity, the smaller screen was designed to be a place to dock productivity tools like Slack or Gmail so they were still readily accessible but less of a distraction while working (or gaming) on the primary display. After convincing Google to open-source the Pebble’s operating system last year, Eric Migicovsky, the creator of what was arguably one of the first truly successful and capable smartwatches, resurrected the wearable under a new company called Core Devices. The Pebble 2 Duo, one of the first smartwatches from Migicovsky’s new company, isn’t as capable as the Apple Watch or devices running Android’s Wear OS, but its careful balance of simplicity, functionality, and a budget-friendly price make it a tempting alternative. It’s still just a prototype and may never find its way into consumers’ homes, but at IFA 2026 in Berlin last week, iRobot debuted the bizarre Roomba Duo. The prototype includes a beefy mobile dock with clean and dirty water tanks and the most powerful floor-cleaning suction the company has ever made. To ensure it can clean all areas in your home, atop the Roomba Duo is a deployable garage with a smaller Roomba Plus 757 inside that can easily fit into tight spaces and beneath furniture. Mere hours before the iPhone Duo made its debut, Retroid revealed the full design of its latest dual-screen handheld it’s been teasing. The Retroid Pocket Duo is a modernized version of the Nintendo DS with dual 120Hz OLED screens featuring a 16:9 aspect ratio on top and a 4:3 aspect ratio between its controls. Full specs and pricing aren’t confirmed yet, but the Pocket Duo features a symmetrical dual thumbstick design, two pairs of shoulder buttons, and a headphone jack. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sign in to see your notifications or create an account to join the conversation.

Automattic CEO Matt Mullenweg placed on leave
2026-09-09

Automattic CEO Matt Mullenweg placed on leave

The Verge | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Mullenweg claims Automattic’s CFO ‘conspired’ with board members to put him on a leave of absence, 404 Media reports. Mullenweg claims Automattic’s CFO ‘conspired’ with board members to put him on a leave of absence, 404 Media reports. Matt Mullenweg, the CEO of WordPress.com owner Automattic, has been placed on a paid leave of absence, as reported earlier by 404 Media. In an internal message by the outlet, Mullenweg claims Automattic chief financial officer Mark Davies “conspired” with board members to place him on leave. “They voted to put me on a paid leave of absence. I voted against that,” Mullenweg wrote, referring to Davies, along with Automattic board members Ann Dunwoody, Toni Schneider, and Sue Decker, 404 Media reports. Mullenweg, who also co-founded WordPress.org, has headed up Automattic since 2014. In 2024, Mullenweg waged a public pressure campaign against a prominent WordPress host, a move that divided the open-source community and ignited a legal battle that’s still going on today. Automattic spokesperson Megan Fox confirmed the company’s decision in an emailed statement to The Verge. “Matt Mullenweg is currently on leave from Automattic,” Fox writes. “Mark Davies, Automattic’s CFO, will lead the company as interim CEO. The Board has full confidence in Mark’s leadership and in the team’s ability to execute against the company’s priorities.” Mullenweg will remain on Automattic’s board, 404 Media reports. “Automattic’s board has decided to ask Matt to step away from his CEO role and take a leave of absence,” Schneider said on the company’s internal message board, according to 404 Media. “We have asked Mark Davies to assume the interim CEO role while Matt is out. We’ve worked closely with Mark over the years and we’re confident he will steer the ship well while Matt is away.” Update, September 9th: Added Automattic’s statement. It’s the year of smartphone price hikes
2026-09-09

It’s the year of smartphone price hikes

The Verge | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Apple, Google, and Samsung’s new phones have two things in common: They include a foldable and they cost more. Apple, Google, and Samsung’s new phones have two things in common: They include a foldable and they cost more. Paying more for a new phone seems almost unavoidable after Apple’s event today. The new iPhone 18 Pro and Pro Max start at $1,199 and $1,299, respectively — a $100 price hike over their predecessors. You can’t dodge the price hike by opting for an older iPhone, either, because Apple raised prices on those by $100, too. And it’s not just Apple. This year, the $100 price hike has been a trend across the industry. Google and Samsung followed the same formula as Apple when they each launched new phones over the past few months. The Pixel 11 starts at $899, up from $799 for last year’s Pixel 10. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra launched at $2,099, $100 more than the Galaxy Z Fold 7 it replaced. You could try looking for a phone from a brand outside the big three, but the price hikes persist there, too. Sony’s new Xperia 10 VIII costs £150 more, and this year’s Moto Razr phones also cost $100 more than their predecessors, each offering little to no significant upgrades to go with their higher prices. Smartphone sales data shows that the market for phones under $100 may be on its way to completely disappearing. The price hikes stem from ongoing component shortages, driven by AI data center buildouts, that could last years and aren’t showing any signs of letting up. RAM price hikes have been among the most severe. Nothing CEO Carl Pei said earlier this year that memory costs for the Nothing Phone 4A had doubled by the time it launched. Nearly every component that goes into new phones, and other tech, is also getting pricier. Qualcomm, which makes the processors that power many new phones, raised prices on its chips earlier this month. Apple was fashionably late to raise its phone prices, like it was late to launch a foldable, potentially signaling that both trends are here to stay. Suno releases its first AI music model made with record industry help
2026-09-09

Suno releases its first AI music model made with record industry help

The Verge | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
v6 still can’t capture the ‘natural imperfections’ of real human music. v6 still can’t capture the ‘natural imperfections’ of real human music. Suno’s new v6 AI music model is its first made with support from the record industry. Suno’s Jack Brody told The Verge that v6 was “trained from the ground up, with a new set of data that does not include the same data that our previous models were trained on.” The data includes content licensed from partners Warner Music Group, BMG, and Believe, as well as “user data.” It’s unclear whether that means v6 training data is completely free of dubiously obtained content. One of the big changes in v6 is that there are actually three different models: v6, v6-wild, and v6-mini. Mini is the model available for free to all. It’s focused on fast, resource-light creation. The results it spits out are simpler and often have more artifacts that betray it as the product of an AI prompt. v6-wild is supposed to be more unpredictable. Brody said that v6-wild is designed for “happy accidents and natural imperfections.” Though, in my limited testing, I found it hard to notice a difference between wild and bog-standard v6. All three models are dramatically better at understanding genre. I recycled a handful of prompts that I used with older models calling for “hyperpop” or “krautrock” songs that missed the mark by a mile. v6, on the other hand, nailed all the superficial hallmarks of any genre I threw at it. What it remains incapable of doing, however, is actually delivering on the “natural imperfections.” As with v5, my attempts to get off-key, out-of-tune, or slightly dissonant results failed every time. No matter how hard you push Suno’s v6, it remains incapable of making anything other than harmonic and rhythmic perfection. Tell it you want “monotone vocals” and “no drums,” and it straight-up ignores you (see below). Repeated demands for “out-of-tune piano played haphazardly, with no regard for key or rhythm” that was “fully out of tune with the rest of the song,” delivered nothing of the sort. It does retain the unnatural imperfections of AI music, with vocals especially seeming to produce more of the harsh-edged artifacts common with AI models than v5 did. Suno’s v6 also has some under-the-hood updates that can fundamentally change how people interact with it. Now users can edit parts of a song using plain language within a chat, and changing individual elements like a guitar line or a single lyric doesn’t require regenerating the entire track. v6 can also mash together multiple elements from your Suno library, combining outputs into new creations. Prompts are also no longer limited to text descriptions. Suno v6 can create tracks based on images, video, or other audio. Suno v6 starts rolling out today, and the company says it will eventually retire its previous models. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Batteries just broke another record in the US
2026-09-09

Batteries just broke another record in the US

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Battery installations hit a new record in the US in the second quarter of 2026. In total, 20.2 gigawatt-hours of new capacity came online, according to a new report. That’s enough to supply the daily electricity needs of about 700,000 homes. The surge is putting the country on a trajectory to see 71 gigawatt-hours of batteries installed in 2026, a 20% increase over last year. This growth is being driven by a combination of cheaper batteries and an urgent need for more energy storage capacity as renewables such as solar and onshore wind power are added to the grid. Massive, utility-scale systems are leading the way; they’re responsible for most of the record-setting quarter. Seven new gigascale battery installations (those with a capacity of over one gigawatt-hour) came online during the three-month stretch, according to the report, published by Benchmark Mineral Intelligence and the Solar Energy Industries Association. “It really came down to a handful of big projects,” says Shan Tomouk, energy storage and energy lead for Benchmark Mineral Intelligence. But there was also growth in the category of so-called behind-the-meter batteries, which include both residential and industrial battery storage systems. These projects, generally smaller than utility-scale installations, are typically owned and operated by homeowners or businesses rather than utilities or power providers. In the behind-the-meter category, data centers led the way, making up about three-quarters of new batteries in the commercial sector. But residential batteries saw a sharp slowdown. These systems are often installed in homes to store power from solar panels or serve as a backup source in case of a blackout. Home installations are projected to drop by 16% in 2026 compared with last year, according to the report. That drop happened largely because a tax credit that helped subsidize home battery systems ended in 2025, Tomouk says. Home installations should recover by the end of the decade, he adds. And tax credits for nonresidential batteries have largely survived. Overall, batteries are a bright spot in energy right now. “This is one of the strong sectors in the US,” says Isshu Kikuma, an energy storage analyst at BloombergNEF, an energy consultancy. As the battery market continues to grow, one major trend to keep an eye on is a move toward US-made technology. Today, nearly all the systems coming online use cells made in China, though some are put together into complete energy storage systems in the US. Tariffs were already pushing the US energy storage industry toward domestic production. And beginning this year, energy storage tax credits required projects to limit their reliance on batteries imported from China. There’s a lot of manufacturing capacity set to come online in the US, though these factories probably won’t be able to meet demand until at least 2030 or so, Tomouk says, so prices could tick up. A key tax credit in the US ended, but heat pump sales are still going strong. El Niño? Climate change? All of the above? Companies are developing new enrichment technology that could unlock previously overlooked resources. Startup Switch Bioworks hopes to use microbes to reduce demand for chemical fertilizer.
